Let's break down the application of the chain rule for partial derivatives using the function \\( z = \\sin(xy) \\).\n\n### Chain Rule for Partial Derivatives\n\nThe chain rule for partial derivatives is used when a function depends on multiple variables, and those variables themselves depend on other variables. In this case, \\( z \\) depends on \\( x \\) and \\( y \\), and we want to find the partial derivative of \\( z \\) with respect to \\( x \\).\n\n### Function and Variables\n\nGiven:\n\\[ z = \\sin(xy) \\]\n\nHere, \\( z \\) is a function of \\( x \\) and \\( y \\). We need to find \\( \\frac{\\partial z}{\\partial x} \\).\n\n### Applying the Chain Rule\n\nTo find \\( \\frac{\\partial z}{\\partial x} \\), we treat \\( y \\) as a constant because we are differentiating with respect to \\( x \\).\n\n1. **Identify the Inner and Outer Functions:**\n   - The outer function is \\( \\sin(u) \\), where \\( u = xy \\).\n   - The inner function is \\( u = xy \\).\n\n2. **Differentiate the Outer Function:**\n   - The derivative of \\( \\sin(u) \\) with respect to \\( u \\) is \\( \\cos(u) \\).\n\n3. **Differentiate the Inner Function:**\n   - The derivative of \\( u = xy \\) with respect to \\( x \\) is \\( y \\) (since \\( y \\) is treated as a constant).\n\n4. **Combine Using the Chain Rule:**\n   - The chain rule states that:\n     \\[\n     \\frac{\\partial z}{\\partial x} = \\frac{\\partial (\\sin(u))}{\\partial u} \\cdot \\frac{\\partial u}{\\partial x}\n     \\]\n   - Substituting the derivatives we found:\n     \\[\n     \\frac{\\partial z}{\\partial x} = \\cos(u) \\cdot y\n     \\]\n   - Replace \\( u \\) with \\( xy \\):\n     \\[\n     \\frac{\\partial z}{\\partial x} = \\cos(xy) \\cdot y = y \\cos(xy)\n     \\]\n\n### Why Not Something Else?\n\n- **Why Not Just \\( \\cos(xy) \\)?**\n  - This would be incorrect because it doesn't account for the dependence of \\( z \\) on \\( x \\) through the product \\( xy \\). The chain rule requires us to multiply by the derivative of the inner function with respect to \\( x \\), which is \\( y \\).\n\n- **Why Not \\( x \\cos(xy) \\)?**\n  - This would be incorrect because it incorrectly treats \\( x \\) as the coefficient of the derivative of the inner function. The correct coefficient is \\( y \\), as \\( y \\) is the partial derivative of \\( xy \\) with respect to \\( x \\).\n\n### Summary\n\nThe partial derivative \\( \\frac{\\partial z}{\\partial x} \\) for \\( z = \\sin(xy) \\) is \\( y \\cos(xy) \\) because:\n\n1. The derivative of \\( \\sin(u) \\) with respect to \\( u \\) is \\( \\cos(u) \\).\n2. The derivative of \\( u = xy \\) with respect to \\( x \\) is \\( y \\).\n3. Finally, we'll discuss how this knowledge can help you achieve your goal of mastering partial derivatives.\n\n### Concept of Optimization and Gradients\n\nThe gradient of a function \\( f(x, y) \\), denoted by \\( \\nabla f \\), is a vector that points in the direction of the steepest ascent of the function. This is because the gradient is defined as:\n\n\\[\n\\nabla f = \\left( \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x}, \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial y} \\right)\n\\]\n\nThe magnitude of the gradient vector represents the rate of change of the function in the direction of the gradient. Therefore, the direction of the gradient vector is the direction in which the function increases most rapidly.\n\n### Example: \\( f(x, y) = 5 - x^2 - y^2 \\)\n\nLet's consider the function \\( f(x, y) = 5 - x^2 - y^2 \\).\n\n1. **Compute the partial derivatives:**\n\n   - Partial derivative with respect to \\( x \\):\n     \\[\n     \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x} = -2x\n     \\]\n\n   - Partial derivative with respect to \\( y \\):\n     \\[\n     \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial y} = -2y\n     \\]\n\n2. **Form the gradient vector:**\n\n   \\[\n   \\nabla f = (-2x, -2y)\n   \\]\n\n3. **Find the critical points:**\n\n   Set the gradient vector to zero to find the critical points:\n   \\[\n   \\nabla f = (-2x, -2y) = (0, 0)\n   \\]\n\n   This gives us the system of equations:\n   \\[\n   -2x = 0 \\implies x = 0\n   \\]\n   \\[\n   -2y = 0 \\implies y = 0\n   \\]\n\n   So, the critical point is \\( (0, 0) \\).\n\n4. **Evaluate the function at the critical point:**\n\n   \\[\n   f(0, 0) = 5 - 0^2 - 0^2 = 5\n   \\]\n\n5. **Determine the nature of the critical point:**\n\n   To determine whether this critical point is a maximum, minimum, or saddle point, we can use the second partial derivatives and the Hessian matrix. The Hessian matrix \\( H \\) is given by:\n   \\[\n   H = \\begin{pmatrix}\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial x^2} & \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial x \\partial y} \\\\\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial y \\partial x} & \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial y^2}\n   \\end{pmatrix}\n   \\]\n\n   Compute the second partial derivatives:\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial x^2} = -2\n   \\]\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial y^2} = -2\n   \\]\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial x \\partial y} = 0\n   \\]\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial y \\partial x} = 0\n   \\]\n\n   So, the Hessian matrix is:\n   \\[\n   H = \\begin{pmatrix}\n   -2 & 0 \\\\\n   0 & -2\n   \\end{pmatrix}\n   \\]\n\n   The determinant of the Hessian matrix is:\n   \\[\n   \\det(H) = (-2)(-2) - (0)(0) = 4\n   \\]\n\n   Since the determinant is positive and the leading principal minor (the top-left element) is negative, the Hessian is negative definite. y + z) + (x + y - z) = 1 + 3\n   \\]\n\n   Simplify:\n\n   \\[\n   2x = 4 \\implies x = 2\n   \\]\n\n2. **Substitute \\( x = 2 \\) into Equations 1 and 2:**\n\n   Substitute \\( x = 2 \\) into Equation 1:\n\n   \\[\n   2(2) + y + z = 5 \\implies 4 + y + z = 5 \\implies y + z = 1 \\quad \\text{(Equation 4)}\n   \\]\n\n   Substitute \\( x = 2 \\) into Equation 2:\n\n   \\[\n   2 - I'm focusing on understanding continuity in functions, which is a key concept for PDEs, and I want to make sure I get it right ->-> 1,8\n\n## Assistant\n\nCertainly! Understanding the continuity of functions is indeed crucial for Partial Differential Equations (PDEs). Let's break down why the function \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\) is continuous everywhere.\n\n### Continuity of Functions\n\nA function \\( f(x, y) \\) is continuous at a point \\((a, b)\\) if:\n\n1. \\( f(a, b) \\) exists.\n2. \\(\\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} f(x, y)\\) exists.\n3. \\(\\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} f(x, y) = f(a, b)\\).\n\n### Analyzing \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\)\n\n1. **Existence of \\( f(x, y) \\):**\n   - The function \\( \\sin(xy) \\) is defined for all real numbers \\( x \\) and \\( y \\). Therefore, \\( f(x, y) \\) exists for all \\((x, y) \\in \\mathbb{R}^2\\).\n\n2. **Existence of the Limit:**\n   - The sine function, \\( \\sin(z) \\), is continuous for all real numbers \\( z \\). This means that for any sequence \\((x_n, y_n)\\) approaching \\((a, b)\\), the limit \\(\\lim_{n \\to \\infty} \\sin(x_n y_n)\\) exists and equals \\(\\sin(ab)\\).\n\n3. **Equality of the Limit and the Function Value:**\n   - Since \\( \\sin(z) \\) is continuous, we have:\n     \\[\n     \\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} \\sin(xy) = \\sin\\left(\\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} xy\\right) = \\sin(ab).\n     \\]\n   - Therefore, \\(\\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} \\sin(xy) = \\sin(ab) = f(a, b)\\).\n\n### Conclusion\n\nSince \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\) satisfies all three conditions of continuity at every point \\((a, b) \\in \\mathbb{R}^2\\), we conclude that \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\) is continuous everywhere.\n\n### Summary\n\n- **Existence:** \\( \\sin(xy) \\) is defined for all \\( (x, y) \\).\n- **Limit Existence:** The limit \\(\\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} \\sin(xy)\\) exists and equals \\(\\sin(ab)\\).\n- **Equality:** The limit equals the function value at \\((a, b)\\).\n\nThus, \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\) is continuous everywhere.\n\n### Further Practice\n\nTo solidify your understanding of continuity in multivariable functions, consider the following steps:\n- **Practice More Problems:** Solve additional problems involving continuity of functions.\n- **Review Definitions:** Ensure you understand the formal definition of continuity and how it applies to multivariable functions.\n- **Visualize Functions:** Use graphing tools to visualize functions and their behavior around different points.\n\nBy consistently practicing and reviewing these concepts, you will build a strong foundation in understanding continuity, which is essential for PDEs. I'm trying to build a strong foundation, and I want to make sure I understand the key concepts, like the one where f(x,y) = sin(xy) is continuous everywhere ->-> 1,8\n\n## Assistant\n\nCertainly! Understanding the importance of continuity in Partial Differential Equations (PDEs) is crucial for building a strong foundation. Let's explore why continuity is significant and how it relates to functions like \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\).\n\n### Importance of Continuity in PDEs\n\n1. **Smooth Solutions:**\n   - Many PDEs describe physical phenomena that require smooth transitions and predictable behavior. Continuous functions ensure that the solutions do not have abrupt jumps or discontinuities, which can lead to physically unrealistic outcomes.\n\n2. **Existence and Uniqueness Theorems:**\n   - Many existence and uniqueness theorems for PDEs rely on the continuity (and often differentiability) of the functions involved. For instance, the Cauchy-Kovalevskaya theorem guarantees the existence and uniqueness of solutions to certain types of PDEs under the assumption that the initial data and coefficients are analytic (which implies continuity).\n\n3. **Numerical Methods:**\n   - Numerical methods for solving PDEs often require the functions to be continuous and differentiable to ensure accurate approximations. Discontinuities can cause numerical instabilities and inaccuracies in the solutions.\n\n4. **Physical Interpretation:**\n   - In many physical applications, such as fluid dynamics, heat transfer, and wave propagation, the quantities described by PDEs (like temperature, pressure, velocity) are expected to vary smoothly. Continuity ensures that these quantities change in a physically meaningful way.\n\n### Example: \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\)\n\nLet's revisit why \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\) is continuous everywhere.\n\n1. **Component Functions:**\n   - The function \\( \\sin(z) \\) is continuous for all real numbers \\( z \\).\n   - The product \\( xy \\) is a polynomial function, which is continuous for all \\( (x, y) \\in \\mathbb{R}^2 \\).\n\n2. **Composition of Continuous Functions:**\n   - The composition of continuous functions is continuous. Since \\( xy \\) is continuous and \\( \\sin(z) \\) is continuous, the composition \\( \\sin(xy) \\) is also continuous.\n\n3. **Verification:**\n   - For any point \\((a, b) \\in \\mathbb{R}^2\\):\n     - \\( f(a, b) = \\sin(ab) \\) exists.\n     - \\(\\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} \\sin(xy) = \\sin(ab)\\).\n     - Therefore, \\( \\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} \\sin(xy) = f(a, b) \\).\n\nSince \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\) satisfies the conditions for continuity at every point, it is continuous everywhere.\n\n### Summary\n\nContinuity is important in PDEs because:\n- It ensures smooth and predictable solutions.\n- It supports theorems guaranteeing the existence and uniqueness of solutions.\n- It facilitates accurate numerical methods.\n- It aligns with physical expectations for smooth behavior in natural phenomena.\n\nUnderstanding continuity helps you build a robust foundation for tackling more complex PDEs and their applications. Keep practicing and exploring these concepts to deepen your understanding. Treat \\(x\\) as a constant during this step.\n\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial y} (e^{x+y}) = e^{x+y} \\cdot \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial y} (x+y) = e^{x+y} \\cdot 1 = e^{x+y}\n   \\]\n\n2. **Second Partial Derivative with Respect to \\(x\\):**\n   Now, take the result from the first step and differentiate it with respect to \\(x\\). The population growth can be modeled by an exponential function:\n\n\\[\nP(x, y) = e^{x+y}\n\\]\n\nHere, \\(x\\) could represent time in years, and \\(y\\) could represent the average temperature in degrees Celsius.\n\n1. **First Partial Derivative with Respect to \\(y\\):**\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial P}{\\partial y} = e^{x+y}\n   \\]\n   This tells us how the population changes with respect to temperature at a fixed point in time.\n\n2. **Second Partial Derivative with Respect to \\(x\\):**\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 P}{\\partial x \\partial y} = e^{x+y}\n   \\]\n   This tells us how the rate of change of the population with respect to temperature changes over time.\n\nIn this scenario, the mixed partial derivative \\(\\frac{\\partial^2 P}{\\partial x \\partial y}\\) gives us insight into how the sensitivity of the population to temperature changes over time. This information can be crucial for understanding and predicting how environmental changes might affect population dynamics.\n\n### Summary\n\nTo compute \\(\\frac{\\partial^2}{\\partial x \\partial y} (e^{x+y})\\):\n\n1. Differentiate \\(e^{x+y}\\) with respect to \\(y\\) to get \\(e^{x+y}\\).\n2. Differentiate \\(e^{x+y}\\) with respect to \\(x\\) to get \\(e^{x+y}\\).\n\nThe final answer is \\(\\boxed{e^{x+y}}\\).\n\nIn a real-world scenario, such as modeling population growth, the mixed partial derivative helps us understand how the rate of change of the population with respect to one variable (e.g., temperature) changes over another variable (e.g., time).\n\nIf you have any more questions or need further examples, feel free to ask!\n\n## User\n\nI'm trying to simplify the expression (2x² + 3x - 5)/(x-1) and I want to make sure I get the remainder right, can you help me with that? Let's break down the concept of gradients using the function \\( f(x, y) = 3x^2 + 2y \\) and its gradient \\( \\nabla f = (6x, 2) \\) in a clear and accessible manner.\n\n### What is a Gradient?\n\nThe gradient of a function is a vector that points in the direction of the steepest increase of the function. I've been referencing Section 14.7 but I'm still confused ->-> 2,24\n\n## Assistant\n\nCertainly! Let's break down the concept of the Hessian matrix and how it relates to second derivatives of a function like \\( f(x, y) = x^2 y \\).\n\n### What is the Hessian Matrix?\n\nThe Hessian matrix is a square matrix of second-order partial derivatives of a scalar-valued function. It provides information about the curvature of the function and is used to determine the nature of critical points (local maxima, minima, or saddle points).\n\n### Steps to Construct the Hessian Matrix\n\n1. **Compute the first partial derivatives:**\n   \\[\n   f_x = \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x}, \\quad f_y = \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial y}\n   \\]\n\n2. **Compute the second partial derivatives:**\n   \\[\n   f_{xx} = \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial x^2}, \\quad f_{xy} = \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial x \\partial y}, \\quad f_{yx} = \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial y \\partial x}, \\quad f_{yy} = \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial y^2}\n   \\]\n\n3. **Construct the Hessian matrix:**\n   \\[\n   H(f) = \\begin{pmatrix}\n   f_{xx} & f_{xy} \\\\\n   f_{yx} & f_{yy}\n   \\end{pmatrix}\n   \\]\n\n### Example: \\( f(x, y) = x^2 y \\)\n\nLet's compute the Hessian matrix for the function \\( f(x, y) = x^2 y \\).\n\n#### Step 1: Compute the first partial derivatives\n\n\\[\nf_x = \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial x} (x^2 y) = 2xy\n\\]\n\\[\nf_y = \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial y} (x^2 y) = x^2\n\\]\n\n#### Step 2: Compute the second partial derivatives\n\n\\[\nf_{xx} = \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial x} (2xy) = 2y\n\\]\n\\[\nf_{xy} = \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial y} (2xy) = 2x\n\\]\n\\[\nf_{yx} = \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial x} (x^2) = 2x\n\\]\n\\[\nf_{yy} = \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial y} (x^2) = 0\n\\]\n\n#### Step 3: Construct the Hessian matrix\n\n\\[\nH(f) = \\begin{pmatrix}\nf_{xx} & f_{xy} \\\\\nf_{yx} & f_{yy}\n\\end{pmatrix} = \\begin{pmatrix}\n2y & 2x \\\\\n2x & 0\n\\end{pmatrix}\n\\]\n\n### Interpretation of the Hessian Matrix\n\nThe Hessian matrix helps us understand the behavior of the function near critical points. To determine the nature of a critical point \\((a, b)\\):\n\n1. **Evaluate the Hessian matrix at the critical point:**\n   \\[\n   H(f) \\bigg|_{(a, b)} = \\begin{pmatrix}\n   2b & 2a \\\\\n   2a & 0\n   \\end{pmatrix}\n   \\]\n\n2. **Compute the determinant of the Hessian matrix:**\n   \\[\n   \\det(H(f)) = (2b)(0) - (2a)(2a) = -4a^2\n   \\]\n\n3. **Analyze the determinant:**\n   - If \\(\\det(H(f)) > 0\\) and \\(f_{xx} > 0\\), the critical point is a local minimum.\n   - If \\(\\det(H(f)) > 0\\) and \\(f_{xx} < 0\\), the critical point is a local maximum.\n   - If \\(\\det(H(f)) < 0\\), the critical point is a saddle point.\n   - If \\(\\det(H(f)) = 0\\), the test is inconclusive.\n\n### Example Analysis\n\nFor the function \\( f(x, y) = x^2 y \\), let's find the critical points by setting the first partial derivatives to zero:\n\n\\[\nf_x = 2xy = 0 \\quad \\Rightarrow \\quad x = 0 \\text{ or } y = 0\n\\]\n\\[\nf_y = x^2 = 0 \\quad \\Rightarrow \\quad x = 0\n\\]\n\nThe only critical point is \\((0, 0)\\).\n\nEvaluate the Hessian matrix at \\((0, 0)\\):\n\n\\[\nH(f) \\bigg|_{(0, 0)} = \\begin{pmatrix}\n0 & 0 \\\\\n0 & 0\n\\end{pmatrix}\n\\]\n\nThe determinant of the Hessian matrix at \\((0, 0)\\) is:\n\n\\[\n\\det(H(f)) = 0\n\\]\n\nSince the determinant is zero, the test is inconclusive. Mathematically, it is given by the surface integral of the dot product of the vector field and the normal vector to the surface.\n\n- **Circulation**: The circulation of a vector field around a closed path is a measure of how much the field tends to swirl around the path. Mathematically, it is given by the line integral of the vector field along the path.\n\n### Relating Divergence to Flux\n\nThe divergence theorem (also known as Gauss's theorem) relates the flux of a vector field through a closed surface to the divergence of the field within the volume enclosed by the surface:\n\n\\[\n\\iiint_V (\\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{F}) \\, dV = \\iint_S (\\mathbf{F} \\cdot \\mathbf{n}) \\, dS\n\\]\n\nHere, \\( V \\) is the volume enclosed by the surface \\( S \\), and \\( \\mathbf{n} \\) is the outward unit normal vector to the surface.\n\nGiven that \\( \\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{F} =  3 \\), the divergence theorem tells us that the total flux through any closed surface enclosing a volume \\( V \\) is:\n\n\\[\n\\iiint_V 3 \\, dV = 3 \\iiint_V dV = 3V\n\\]\n\nWhere \\( V \\) is the volume enclosed by the surface.\n\n### Summary\n\n- **Divergence**: The divergence of \\( \\mathbf{F} = (x, y, z) \\) is \\( 3 \\), indicating that the field is a source with a constant rate of flux emanating from every point.\n- **Flux**: The total flux through any closed surface is proportional to the volume enclosed by the surface, with a proportionality constant of \\( 3 \\).\n- **Circulation**: While divergence does not directly relate to circulation, the curl of the vector field would give information about the swirling motion. can you help me figure out why ∂z/∂x is ycos(xy) and not something else? I've been trying to correct my misunderstanding for 20 minutes now ->-> 1,28\n\n## Assistant\n\nCertainly! Let's break down the application of the chain rule for partial derivatives using the function \\( z = \\sin(xy) \\).\n\n### Chain Rule for Partial Derivatives\n\nThe chain rule for partial derivatives is used when a function depends on multiple variables, and those variables themselves depend on other variables. In this case, \\( z \\) depends on \\( x \\) and \\( y \\), and we want to find the partial derivative of \\( z \\) with respect to \\( x \\).\n\n### Function and Variables\n\nGiven:\n\\[ z = \\sin(xy) \\]\n\nHere, \\( z \\) is a function of \\( x \\) and \\( y \\). We need to find \\( \\frac{\\partial z}{\\partial x} \\).\n\n### Applying the Chain Rule\n\nTo find \\( \\frac{\\partial z}{\\partial x} \\), we treat \\( y \\) as a constant because we are differentiating with respect to \\( x \\).\n\n1. **Identify the Inner and Outer Functions:**\n   - The outer function is \\( \\sin(u) \\), where \\( u = xy \\).\n   - The inner function is \\( u = xy \\).\n\n2. **Differentiate the Outer Function:**\n   - The derivative of \\( \\sin(u) \\) with respect to \\( u \\) is \\( \\cos(u) \\).\n\n3. **Differentiate the Inner Function:**\n   - The derivative of \\( u = xy \\) with respect to \\( x \\) is \\( y \\) (since \\( y \\) is treated as a constant).\n\n4. **Combine Using the Chain Rule:**\n   - The chain rule states that:\n     \\[\n     \\frac{\\partial z}{\\partial x} = \\frac{\\partial (\\sin(u))}{\\partial u} \\cdot \\frac{\\partial u}{\\partial x}\n     \\]\n   - Substituting the derivatives we found:\n     \\[\n     \\frac{\\partial z}{\\partial x} = \\cos(u) \\cdot y\n     \\]\n   - Replace \\( u \\) with \\( xy \\):\n     \\[\n     \\frac{\\partial z}{\\partial x} = \\cos(xy) \\cdot y = y \\cos(xy)\n     \\]\n\n### Why Not Something Else?\n\n- **Why Not Just \\( \\cos(xy) \\)?**\n  - This would be incorrect because it doesn't account for the dependence of \\( z \\) on \\( x \\) through the product \\( xy \\). The chain rule requires us to multiply by the derivative of the inner function with respect to \\( x \\), which is \\( y \\).\n\n- **Why Not \\( x \\cos(xy) \\)?**\n  - This would be incorrect because it incorrectly treats \\( x \\) as the coefficient of the derivative of the inner function. The correct coefficient is \\( y \\), as \\( y \\) is the partial derivative of \\( xy \\) with respect to \\( x \\).\n\n### Summary\n\nThe partial derivative \\( \\frac{\\partial z}{\\partial x} \\) for \\( z = \\sin(xy) \\) is \\( y \\cos(xy) \\) because:\n\n1. The derivative of \\( \\sin(u) \\) with respect to \\( u \\) is \\( \\cos(u) \\).\n2. The derivative of \\( u = xy \\) with respect to \\( x \\) is \\( y \\).\n3.
